Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a big amount all at once. I mean, I might pick at something here or there, but within an hour, like really a lot? No, I can’t say I’ve noticed that happening. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt upset after eating sometimes, but I don’t go and make myself throw up. I wouldn’t want to do that. Is that what you were asking?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you limit the types or amounts of food you eat?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Restrictive_Intake\nmanifestation: \"I cook plates for my husband and my mother-in-law, but by the time I sit down I usually tell myself coffee, a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast is enough for me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I… yes, I suppose I do, though I try not to make a fuss about it. I cook proper plates for my husband and my mother-in-law, you know, normal meals. But by the time I sit down myself, I usually think, well, coffee is enough, or just a few vegetables… maybe half a piece of toast.

I’m usually quite careful, maybe too careful, so it’s hard to imagine really losing control.\n\nThere are times I feel uncomfortable after eating something I hadn’t planned, but I wouldn’t call it a binge or anything like that. I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose I do. Not all the time, but if I eat something heavier—like biscuits or pasta—I get nervous afterward. I’ll keep thinking, “Is that going to show on me?” and I end up checking myself the rest of the day.\n\nI know that may sound silly… I just feel like I need to make up for it somehow. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a big amount all at once. I mean, I might pick at something here or there, but within an hour, like really a lot? No, I can’t say I’ve noticed that happening. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt upset after eating sometimes, but I don’t go and make myself throw up. I wouldn’t want to do that. Is that what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you limit the types or amounts of food you eat?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I suppose I do, though I try not to make a fuss about it. I cook proper plates for my husband and my mother-in-law, you know, normal meals. But by the time I sit down myself, I usually think, well, coffee is enough, or just a few vegetables… maybe half a piece of toast.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like I’m doing anything drastic in the moment. I tell myself I don’t need much. Is that what you mean by limiting?\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s all day exactly… but yes, it does come up a lot. I notice how I look, especially around my stomach. If I feel small and neat, I feel like I’m doing okay, like I’ve got things under control.\n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach seems even a little full, I get this awful feeling like I’ve let myself go. I know that probably sounds silly… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be extremely upset if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I would be upset. Maybe more than I should be, if I’m honest.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me. I keep sort of checking myself, and thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I know a small amount probably shouldn’t matter that much… but it feels like it does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people mean, I suppose. I’m usually quite careful, maybe too careful, so it’s hard to imagine really losing control.\n\nThere are times I feel uncomfortable after eating something I hadn’t planned, but I wouldn’t call it a binge or anything like that. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, there has been some weight loss. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my doctor did say I’ve gotten too thin.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed my work pants are hanging looser on me than they used to. Part of me knows that should worry me, but… if I’m being honest, when the number on the scale is lower, I still feel a bit relieved. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit the food you eat mostly driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… though I don’t like saying it that plainly.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can get scared it’s going to show on me, and then I spend the rest of the day checking myself or thinking I should make up for it somehow. I know that probably sounds a bit silly, but yes… I think fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I limit things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Oh… well, I’m not really sure that applies to me now, at my age. I don’t have periods anymore, so I can’t say I’ve noticed changes in that way.\n\nIs that what you meant, or were you asking more generally about hormones and things?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.0", "F50.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

But by the time I sit down myself, I usually think, well, coffee is enough, or just a few vegetables… maybe half a piece of toast.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like I’m doing anything drastic in the moment. I tell myself I don’t need much. Is that what you mean by limiting?\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s all day exactly… but yes, it does come up a lot. I notice how I look, especially around my stomach. If I feel small and neat, I feel like I’m doing okay, like I’ve got things under control.\n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach seems even a little full, I get this awful feeling like I’ve let myself go. I know that probably sounds silly… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be extremely upset if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I would be upset. Maybe more than I should be, if I’m honest.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me. I keep sort of checking myself, and thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I know a small amount probably shouldn’t matter that much… but it feels like it does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people mean, I suppose. I’m usually quite careful, maybe too careful, so it’s hard to imagine really losing control.\n\nThere are times I feel uncomfortable after eating something I hadn’t planned, but I wouldn’t call it a binge or anything like that. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, there has been some weight loss. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my doctor did say I’ve gotten too thin.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed my work pants are hanging looser on me than they used to. Part of me knows that should worry me, but… if I’m being honest, when the number on the scale is lower, I still feel a bit relieved. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit the food you eat mostly driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… though I don’t like saying it that plainly.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can get scared it’s going to show on me, and then I spend the rest of the day checking myself or thinking I should make up for it somehow. I know that probably sounds a bit silly, but yes… I think fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I limit things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Oh… well, I’m not really sure that applies to me now, at my age. I don’t have periods anymore, so I can’t say I’ve noticed changes in that way.\n\nIs that what you meant, or were you asking more generally about hormones and things?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of time where you gained back the weight you had lost?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, not in any lasting way. My doctor keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, and I can see it too, with my work pants hanging loose.\n\nBut I suppose part of me still feels… pleased, or relieved, when the number on the scale is lower. I know that may not be the right way to look at it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my regular doctor thought I should come talk to someone. She says I’ve gotten too thin. I don’t know, I guess my work pants are hanging pretty loose lately, so maybe she’s right.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be difficult or anything… but when the number on the scale is lower, part of me feels kind of relieved. I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose I do. Not all the time, but if I eat something heavier—like biscuits or pasta—I get nervous afterward. I’ll keep thinking, “Is that going to show on me?” and I end up checking myself the rest of the day.\n\nI know that may sound silly… I just feel like I need to make up for it somehow. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a big amount all at once. I mean, I might pick at something here or there, but within an hour, like really a lot? No, I can’t say I’ve noticed that happening. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt upset after eating sometimes, but I don’t go and make myself throw up. I wouldn’t want to do that. Is that what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you limit the types or amounts of food you eat?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I suppose I do, though I try not to make a fuss about it. I cook proper plates for my husband and my mother-in-law, you know, normal meals. But by the time I sit down myself, I usually think, well, coffee is enough, or just a few vegetables… maybe half a piece of toast.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like I’m doing anything drastic in the moment. I tell myself I don’t need much. Is that what you mean by limiting?\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s all day exactly… but yes, it does come up a lot. I notice how I look, especially around my stomach. If I feel small and neat, I feel like I’m doing okay, like I’ve got things under control.\n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach seems even a little full, I get this awful feeling like I’ve let myself go. I know that probably sounds silly… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be extremely upset if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I would be upset. Maybe more than I should be, if I’m honest.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me. I keep sort of checking myself, and thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I know a small amount probably shouldn’t matter that much… but it feels like it does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people mean, I suppose. I’m usually quite careful, maybe too careful, so it’s hard to imagine really losing control.\n\nThere are times I feel uncomfortable after eating something I hadn’t planned, but I wouldn’t call it a binge or anything like that. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, there has been some weight loss. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my doctor did say I’ve gotten too thin.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed my work pants are hanging looser on me than they used to. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit the food you eat mostly driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… though I don’t like saying it that plainly.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can get scared it’s going to show on me, and then I spend the rest of the day checking myself or thinking I should make up for it somehow. I know that may sound a bit harsh, and I’m not trying to be difficult… that’s just how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you gained back a significant amount of weight after losing it?\n\nPatient: No, not a significant amount, I don’t think. My doctor keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, and I can see my work pants are hanging loose on me.\n\nI suppose… I haven’t really gained it back, not in a way that lasts. And I feel a little embarrassed saying this, but part of me still feels relieved when the scale is lower. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you look fat even when you are at a weight that others consider too thin?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, but if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror I feel like I've let myself go.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say “fat,” exactly… that feels a little strong. But when I’m small and neat-looking, I feel like I’m handling things the right way.

If I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I do feel like I’ve let myself go. I know that probably isn’t the best way to think about it, but… yes, that’s how it feels to me.

I mean, I might pick at something here or there, but within an hour, like really a lot? No, I can’t say I’ve noticed that happening. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I keep sort of checking myself, and thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I suppose it is part of it, yes… though I don’t like saying it that bluntly.

If I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me. Then I keep checking myself, or thinking I ought to make up for it somehow. I know that probably sounds silly, but that fear is definitely there.

If I feel small and neat, I feel like I’m doing okay, like I’ve got things under control.\n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach seems even a little full, I get this awful feeling like I’ve let myself go. I know that probably sounds silly… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be extremely upset if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I would be upset. Maybe more than I should be, if I’m honest.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can spend the rest of the day worrying it’s going to show on me. I keep sort of checking myself, and thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I know a small amount probably shouldn’t matter that much… but it feels like it does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people mean, I suppose. I’m usually quite careful, maybe too careful, so it’s hard to imagine really losing control.\n\nThere are times I feel uncomfortable after eating something I hadn’t planned, but I wouldn’t call it a binge or anything like that. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, there has been some weight loss. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my doctor did say I’ve gotten too thin.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed my work pants are hanging looser on me than they used to. Part of me knows that should worry me, but… if I’m being honest, when the number on the scale is lower, I still feel a bit relieved. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit the food you eat mostly driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… though I don’t like saying it that plainly.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I can get scared it’s going to show on me, and then I spend the rest of the day checking myself or thinking I should make up for it somehow. I know that probably sounds a bit silly, but yes… I think fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I limit things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Oh… well, I’m not really sure that applies to me now, at my age. I don’t have periods anymore, so I can’t say I’ve noticed changes in that way.\n\nIs that what you meant, or were you asking more generally about hormones and things?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of time where you gained back the weight you had lost?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, not in any lasting way. My doctor keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, and I can see it too, with my work pants hanging loose.\n\nBut I suppose part of me still feels… pleased, or relieved, when the number on the scale is lower. I know that may not be the right way to look at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I think I look… exactly overweight, no. But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full, I feel like I’ve let myself go. I know that may sound a bit harsh, and I’m not trying to be difficult… that’s just how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you gained back a significant amount of weight after losing it?\n\nPatient: No, not a significant amount, I don’t think. And I feel a little embarrassed saying this, but part of me still feels relieved when the scale is lower. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when you are at a weight that others consider too thin?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “fat,” exactly… that feels a little strong. I don’t have periods anymore, so I can’t say I’ve noticed changes in that way.\n\nIs that what you meant, or were you asking more generally about hormones and things?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of time where you gained back the weight you had lost?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, not in any lasting way. My doctor keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, and I can see it too, with my work pants hanging loose.\n\nBut I suppose part of me still feels… pleased, or relieved, when the number on the scale is lower. I know that may not be the right way to look at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I think I look… exactly overweight, no. But I don’t see what other people seem to see either.\n\nWhen I’m small and neat-looking, I feel like I’m managing myself properly, if that makes sense. But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full, I feel like I’ve let myself go. I know that may sound a bit harsh, and I’m not trying to be difficult… that’s just how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you gained back a significant amount of weight after losing it?\n\nPatient: No, not a significant amount, I don’t think. Then I keep checking myself, or thinking I ought to make up for it somehow. I know that probably sounds silly, but that fear is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your self-worth is closely tied to your weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose it is, more than I like to admit.\n\nWhen I stay small and neat-looking, I feel like I’m… doing things properly, like I’ve got myself together. I know that probably sounds vain or silly, but it does affect how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the most important aspect of your appearance?\n\nPatient: Maybe not the most important… I don’t want to sound as if that’s all I care about.\n\nBut yes, it does matter a lot to me. When I stay small and neat-looking, I feel like I’m handling everything right. And if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I feel like I’ve let myself go. I know that probably isn’t a very sensible way to judge myself, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s the only reason… or at least, I don’t want to make it sound too simple.\n\nBut it is a big part of it.
